a:T5d4,The SEFD pipeline processes 18.5 million SEC filings dating to 1994. First, filings are routed via SGML header metadata to type-specific parsers covering 33 XML schemas and over 350 filing variants (annual reports, quarterly reports, event disclosures, insider trading reports, institutional holdings, etc.). Instead of linear DOM extraction, the parser uses a visual-first design that maps rendered documents to a 2D coordinate grid, reconstructing spatial relationships between text spans to recover indentation hierarchies and join text fragments separated by presentational markup. A dedicated table reconstruction module resolves merged cells, reattaches currency/percent/negative signs to associated numeric values, and fixes common extraction errors including misaligned labels, duplicated headers, and sign mismatches. Parsed content is serialized to lightweight MultiMarkdown, which uses simple syntax to represent table spans while removing all render-only HTML scaffolding. The pipeline includes rule-based quality checks, deduplication, and cross-corpus contamination measurement. Two benchmarks are released alongside the corpus: EDGAR-Forecast uses strict time splits, requiring models to predict values in post-knowledge-cutoff 2026 filings using only pre-cutoff documents to eliminate memorization; EDGAR-OCR uses synthetically modified real tables, with entities, dates, and values replaced while preserving layout and arithmetic consistency, to prevent training set leakage.b:T4e4,No technical approach is presented. The announced partnerships fall into four categories: enterprise engineering deployments — Claude Code rolled out across NAVER's entire engineering organization, used by Nexon for writing, reviewing, and shipping code for live-service games, deployed across LG CNS and Samsung SDS for software development and knowledge work, and delivered via AWS Bedrock at Hanwha Solutions to satisfy in-region data residency and security requirements; startup product integration — Channel Corp embedding Claude into Channel Talk, a customer AI platform serving over 230,000 companies across Korea, Japan, and the US; academic collaboration — Claude access granted to NAIRL researchers with a focus on safety, evaluation, alignment, and robustness; nonprofit use cases — Good Neighbors Korea leveraging Claude for program outcome analysis, social welfare law and internal guideline navigation, and administrative workload reduction. The separate TCS partnership covers three dimensions: Claude provision to 50,000 TCS employees across 56 countries, development of Claude-powered products for regulated industries including financial services, healthcare, and the public sector, and TCS joining the Claude Partner Network.c:T570,Weights are parameterized as zero-mean Gaussian with standard deviation d_in^{-γ}, where γ=0.5 recovers standard Xavier initialization and larger γ corresponds to smaller initial weight scale. Controlled pretraining runs across model sizes from 0.1B to 1.5B first confirm that small initialization reduces validation loss, but gains decay sharply with model size. Two architectural factors suppressing these gains are isolated. First, the RMSNorm stability constant epsilon enters a saturation regime when hidden state variance drops below epsilon, making normalization invariant to weight scale and erasing initialization effects. Second, small initialization exacerbates the attention sink effect — over-concentration of attention weights on the first sequence token — which is mitigated by gated attention. Combining a reduced epsilon value with gated attention restores small initialization benefits across model scales, producing improved scaling behavior. Weight dynamics tracking and token-level loss decomposition identify γ=1 as a critical optimum, where residual and identity pathway contributions are balanced. Small initialization models follow a condense-then-expand trajectory: weights first collapse to a low-dimensional subspace in early training, then gradually expand to high-rank representations, with loss gains concentrated on difficult, reasoning-related tokens.d:T517,Under standard architectural settings (epsilon=1e-5, vanilla attention), moving from γ=0.5 to γ=1 reduces validation loss by ~0.05 at 0.1B scale, but only by ~0.003 at 1.5B scale, showing clear gain decay with size. Ablations on epsilon show that for 1.5B models at γ=1, reducing epsilon from 1e-5 to 1e-12 lowers validation loss by ~0.038; the same epsilon change produces only ~0.001 loss reduction at γ=0.5. Quantitative analysis confirms the saturation effect: for hidden dimension d=2048, hidden state variance at γ=1 is 2.4e-7 (well below epsilon=1e-5), while variance at γ=0.5 is 4.8e-4 (well above saturation). Combining both architectural fixes eliminates gain decay across model sizes, producing a better scaling slope than standard initialization, with largest downstream gains on reasoning tasks. A sweep of γ identifies γ=1 as the optimal tradeoff between stability and performance, with higher γ introducing training instability. Weight dynamics measurements show that γ=1 models exhibit significantly lower effective weight rank (condensation) in early training compared to standard initialization; token-level loss decomposition shows negligible differences on simple high-frequency tokens, with gains concentrated on high-loss, semantically complex tokens requiring reasoning.e:T43a,1. Inference scaling gains are highly benchmark-dependent: hard benchmarks including FrontierMath, TerminalBench, and Humanity's Last Exam continue to show steady performance gains far above default token budgets, while other benchmarks show weaker marginal returns. 2. Newer model generations consistently achieve larger advantages at high inference budgets, meaning low-budget evaluations systematically under-measure newer models' ability to convert extra compute to performance. 3. No universal inference allocation strategy is optimal across tasks: repeated submissions deliver consistent gains across all benchmarks, correctness feedback delivers the largest gains on tasks with clear verifiers that support iterative search (HLE, SWE-Bench Pro), and parallel scaling performs best on stateless, non-interactive QA benchmarks (HealthBench, HLE) and weakest on interactive benchmarks with persistent environment state. 4. Longer trajectories without proper context management cause performance degradation, so raw token budget increases alone cannot deliver full scaling gains.f:T404,The proposed Variable-Width Transformer changes the model dimension by layer. The best-performing profile is ×-shaped: wide at the bottom, narrow in the middle, and wide again near the top. The implementation detail that matters is the residual stream. Instead of inserting learned projections between layers, the model keeps a fixed global residual dimension equal to the widest layer. Each block reads from and writes to a layer-specific slice of this residual stream. When a layer is narrower, unused coordinates bypass the block. When a later layer expands, those coordinates are restored from the most recent layer that actively processed them; if no such value exists, the coordinate is zero-padded. This parameter-free resizing avoids projection bottlenecks and keeps the skip path closer to a standard residual stream. The design differs from work that only reallocates FFN intermediate width: here the full block width changes, so attention, MLP parameters, KV cache, activation movement, and compute are all affected.10:T7bc,The core architecture is a Looped World Model in which a parameter-shared transformer block is applied repeatedly to refine the latent state for a single transition. This creates a two-level structure: an outer temporal evolution over environment steps, and an inner iterative refinement loop for the next latent state. Instead of using a stack of distinct layers once, the model reuses the same transition operator multiple times.

A central technical ingredient is a spectrally constrained state-retention parameterization, adapted from recent work on stabilizing looped transformers. The intuition is straightforward: part of the recurrent update is written as a contractive linear retention term, combined with input injection and transformer nonlinearities. The goal is not to encode exact physics, but to keep the latent dynamics bounded as the number of inner-loop iterations grows. This is a meaningful departure from standard deep transformers. In ordinary residual stacks, stability is mostly handled implicitly through initialization, normalization, and optimization. In a looped model, the same block is reapplied many times, so the spectral properties of the induced state transition become directly tied to whether deeper recurrence remains stable.

The paper also emphasizes adaptive computation. Different transition steps have different difficulty, so a fixed amount of inner-loop compute is wasteful on easy steps and insufficient on hard ones. LoopWM allows the number of refinement iterations to vary, effectively allocating more compute where the transition is harder. That is a substantial modeling difference from conventional fixed-depth world models.

Conceptually, this is not a diffusion world model and not simply a replacement for RSSM or autoregressive transformer dynamics. It adds a shared-depth computational dimension that is orthogonal to scaling width, data, or context. The paper explicitly frames iterative latent depth as a new scaling axis.11:T613,Two headline claims are visible from the provided full text. First, the model achieves up to 100× parameter efficiency relative to conventional approaches. That should be read as a Pareto-style claim—similar modeling quality with far fewer independent parameters—rather than a blanket statement under equal FLOPs. Second, the architecture supports adaptive computation, allowing inference depth to scale with transition complexity.

The excerpt does not include the full experimental tables, so it is not possible to safely reproduce benchmark-specific numbers, environment names, or exact gains over baselines such as Dreamer, IRIS, or EMERALD. The most important experiments to verify in the full paper are likely: (1) comparisons against non-shared deep transformers or RSSM-style baselines at matched parameter budgets; (2) whether increasing loop iterations improves long-horizon rollout quality; (3) whether train-time and test-time loop counts can differ, enabling test-time compute scaling; (4) ablations showing instability or degradation without spectral constraints; and (5) whether adaptive halting improves only efficiency or also final quality.

When reading the full results, the most informative numbers will be: parameter-performance Pareto curves, rollout metrics as a function of loop depth, and statistics showing how adaptive compute is distributed across environments or timesteps. Those are the results that determine whether the paper is merely parameter-efficient or whether it genuinely introduces a useful new compute axis.12:T43a,Read the introduction for the problem framing, then go directly to the method section. The key material is the looped update rule, the state-retention parameterization, the spectral constraint, and the adaptive-computation mechanism. That is where the paper either stands or falls.

In the experiments, prioritize four kinds of figures and tables: (1) parameter-versus-performance tradeoffs; (2) performance as loop count increases; (3) long-horizon rollout degradation curves; and (4) stability ablations, especially with and without the spectral constraint. If the paper includes train-loop versus test-loop cross-evaluation, that deserves special attention because it speaks directly to test-time compute scaling.

Estimated reading time is 25–40 minutes. For readers interested in transfer to LLM pretraining, the main questions are whether the shared-depth recursion is learning an actual iterative computation rather than acting as regularization, and whether the same inner-depth idea could transfer to video token LMs, diffusion transformers, or any-order sequence models.13:T52e,ReLAR augments a pretrained transformer with an iterative latent refinement loop. A normal forward pass produces the final-layer hidden state h0. A lightweight extractor compresses this into a low-dimensional reasoning state s0, which acts as a bottleneck summary of the current internal reasoning status. Instead of decoding immediately, the model performs a sequence of refinement steps in latent space. Two learned controllers govern the process: a depth controller decides how many refinement steps to take, and an action controller chooses the direction or type of refinement at each step. Both controllers consume only the compact reasoning state, which keeps the control mechanism lightweight and avoids direct search over the full hidden tensor. Training uses policy gradients with a step-wise likelihood improvement signal as reward: if a refinement step improves the likelihood of the desired output, it is reinforced. This gives denser credit assignment than using only final-task reward and avoids explicit supervision on natural-language reasoning traces. Relative to prior latent intervention work, the novelty is iterative and adaptive hidden-state revision rather than one-shot editing or static steering. Relative to CoT, the method aims to trade text-level reasoning overhead for internal latent computation.14:T4ad,The method replaces the in_proj and out_proj layers in all 48 Mamba-2 blocks with grouped ternary linear layers. These 96 modules account for about 85.7% of the parameters. Weights are partitioned into groups, mainly with group size 128. For each group, the scale is recomputed as the mean absolute weight value, then weights are clipped, rounded to {-1, 0, +1}, and dequantized by multiplying by the group scale. Gradients pass through a straight-through estimator. The SSM dynamics parameters A_log, D, and dt_bias, as well as conv1d, embeddings, lm_head, and norms, remain FP16 because prior SSM quantization work finds these components sensitive at low precision. Training uses the public FP16 Mamba-2 1.3B checkpoint as initialization and a frozen FP16 teacher for distillation, mixing language-model loss and distillation loss with α=0.5 and temperature 1.0. A key observation is zero-ratio collapse: when group scales are learned parameters, optimization can increase the scale, widen the zero region, push more weights to zero, and reduce capacity in a positive feedback loop. Recomputing absmean scales at every forward pass provides a stabilizing feedback and keeps sparsity around 26%.15:T457,The main run trains for 50k steps on C4 with sequence length 1024, totaling about 102M tokens, on a single H100 for roughly 4 hours. The compressed model reduces storage from 2687 MB to 744 MB, a 3.61x reduction. It reaches 48.1% average zero-shot accuracy over seven tasks, close to Bi-Mamba's 48.4%; the 0.3 percentage-point difference is within the reported approximate ±0.9pp confidence interval. On C4, perplexity increases by 4.20 relative to the FP16 model, about a 24% gap. The WikiText-2 gap is larger and attributed partly to domain mismatch. Naive ternary PTQ gives perplexity around 13 million, effectively unusable, which supports the need for QAT. Multi-seed runs show low variance: about 0.01 standard deviation in C4 perplexity and ±0.13pp in the seven-task average. Extending training to 307M tokens keeps improving C4 perplexity without a visible plateau. The log-token trend suggests further gains, but the extrapolation is based on only four points. Group-size ablations show only about 0.06 PPL range, indicating that the ternary constraint, not scale granularity, is the main bottleneck.16:T564,Benchmark: 1,204 continuation instances from 38 CC-licensed serialized audio dramas, genre-balanced, evaluated at h∈{10,20,50,100,200}. The primary metric is Plot-Beat F1 over a 14-class Save-the-Cat taxonomy extracted by a held-out judge ensemble; eight additional automated metrics cover voice consistency, rule violations from series bibles, foreshadowing payoff, temporal coherence via event-chain ordering, thematic recurrence, emotion-arc alignment (DTW), dialogue attribution F1, and motif persistence. Prompts are professionally translated into four Indic languages with native-speaker cultural-fidelity ratings.
Model (N-VSSM): a Mamba-2 8B decoder augmented with a per-scene latent z_t∈R^256 updated at scene boundaries using an event tuple e_t=(actor, action, object, location, outcome) and the decoder hidden state. Training uses a per-scene negative ELBO with KL annealing plus an auxiliary foreshadowing-payoff loss. At generation time, z_t conditions the decoder through cross-attention into low-rank adapters inserted every 4 Mamba blocks, acting as an explicit global-state pathway.
Cross-lingual transfer: a language-specific residual transform T_l:R^256→R^256 (2-layer MLP) trained on 24k parallel scene pairs with a contrastive objective and divergence penalty, shifting the latent into a target cultural/linguistic region without retraining the decoder.17:T443,Audit of 21 systems (classical, narrative fine-tunes, open frontier, closed frontier, reasoning-tier). At h=50, closed-frontier and reasoning-tier models cluster tightly at Plot-Beat F1 0.78–0.81 with no significant pairwise differences (Welch t-test + Bonferroni, p>0.13). All closed/reasoning systems show a monotonic drop of ~0.18–0.21 F1 from h=10 to h=200 (Bonferroni-corrected p<1e-4), i.e., a long-horizon collapse.
N-VSSM reports Plot-Beat F1 ≥0.84 at all horizons with a near-flat curve, and ~4× lower per-episode inference cost (Table 3). At h=200, it improves long-horizon structural metrics vs the “frontier band”: foreshadowing payoff +0.18, temporal coherence +0.14, motif persistence +0.12. Cultural Transfer Function improves native-speaker cultural-fidelity ratings (7-point Likert) by +0.20 to +0.23 across Hindi/Tamil/Telugu/Marathi. Training details: 480B-token deduplicated English fiction pretrain for the backbone; joint fine-tune with the latent module on 1.8M serialized-fiction scenes with strict series-level held-out splits; 384 H100 GPUs for 9.4 days.18:T81e,ReproRepo has three core pieces.

First, data construction. The authors collect paper–repository pairs from major ML conferences, then mine GitHub issues from the linked repositories. They filter for issues that correspond to reproducibility blockers rather than generic feature requests or discussion. To avoid leaking the answer to the agent, they reconstruct repository snapshots aligned with issue timing and fix timing, such as issue-time snapshots, pre-fix snapshots, and post-fix snapshots. This turns naturally occurring historical failures into hidden evaluation targets.

Second, the task setup. Agents are given the paper and a fixed repository snapshot, but they do not execute code. The setting is intentionally compute-light and limited to static inspection. This is a practical design choice: many modern ML repositories require GPUs, large datasets, and external APIs, so full execution is expensive and hard to standardize. The benchmark therefore asks a narrower but still meaningful question: how many real reproducibility blockers can an agent surface from static inspection alone?

Third, the evaluation protocol. Agent findings are compared against human-reported issues using both exact match and semantic match. Metrics are reported at both the issue level and the paper level, along with cost. This layered evaluation is important because reproducibility failures are often described differently by different people. Exact match alone would understate capability, while semantic match alone would overstate localization quality. The paper also distinguishes visible failures from less visible ones, which helps characterize what current agents are actually good at.

Relative to prior work, the novelty is less about task format and more about supervision source and scale. Instead of expert-authored tasks, it uses naturally occurring GitHub issues. Instead of tens of papers, it covers 1,149 papers and 7,553 human-reported blockers. The trade-off is noisier supervision, but the snapshot design and multi-granularity metrics help manage that noise.19:T537,The scale of the evaluation is the main result. The benchmark covers 1,149 papers from three major ML conferences and 7,553 human-reported reproducibility blockers. The authors evaluate Claude Code and Codex paired with four frontier LLMs. Under the static-inspection setting, the results are stronger than one might expect: Codex with GPT-5.5 finds at least one semantically related hidden issue for 93.3% of NeurIPS 2024 main-conference papers and 89.7% of ICLR 2026 papers.

The more important finding, however, is the gap between semantic coverage and exact localization. Agents are often able to identify the right semantic region of failure, especially for visible problems such as missing imports, broken scripts, dependency mismatches, or obvious setup issues. But they are much weaker at pinning down the exact source of the problem and at identifying less visible failure types. The paper also reports relatively low false positive rates, which suggests the agents are not simply generating broad generic warnings to inflate recall.

When reading the experiments, focus on four things: comparisons across agent/model combinations; the gap between exact match and semantic match; the stratified analysis of visible versus less visible failures; and the cost analysis, since scalability is one of the benchmark’s main claims.1a:T5ea,Read it in the order of data construction, evaluation protocol, then error analysis.

Start with Figure 1 and Table 1. They make the benchmark positioning clear relative to earlier reproducibility evaluations such as PaperBench, CORE-Bench, and REPRO-Bench. The key point is not the names, but why this benchmark scales: supervision comes from GitHub issues rather than expert-authored tasks.

Then read the data construction section carefully. Check how reproducibility-related issues are filtered, how repository snapshots are aligned with issue timing, and how pre-fix versus post-fix states are used. This determines whether the benchmark is really measuring reproducibility blockers rather than repository maintenance noise.

Next, read the metric definitions. Pay close attention to exact match versus semantic match, issue-level versus paper-level metrics, and how false positives are counted. In agent benchmarks, much of the real disagreement usually comes from the matching protocol.

Finally, spend time on the qualitative analysis. The most useful part is the failure analysis: why agents can often identify the right semantic area but fail to localize precisely; which failure types are inherently hard for static inspection; and whether some findings are inferred from the paper text without being grounded in the codebase.

If you only want the main ideas, read the introduction, Figure 1, Table 1, the main result table, and the qualitative error analysis. That should take around 25 to 35 minutes.1b:T4c8,The main contribution is a benchmark construction framework plus validation protocol. The paper first dissects failure modes in prior MIA evaluations. Temporal-cutoff benchmarks such as WikiMIA confound membership with time-dependent changes in writing style and content. Open-model train/validation splits are also problematic when only the training set is deduplicated, because duplicate structure itself becomes a membership cue. Even deduplicating validation against train is not fully sound, since it alters the non-member distribution. Based on this analysis, the authors build cleaner splits for two open LLM families, aiming to keep member and non-member examples matched in source distribution while differing only in whether they were seen before a fixed training point. They then run blind baselines—classifiers with no access to the target model, using only the raw text—to verify that the split is not trivially separable. Only after this sanity check do they benchmark standard MIA methods and report metrics such as AUC and TPR at low FPR. The strongest part of the paper is its treatment of benchmark validity: it makes the i.i.d. assumption explicit and turns it into an operational evaluation protocol.1c:T405,The proposed structural uncertainty framework quantifies consistency via the stability of self-preference-induced rankings over a model's own sampled reasoning solutions. The pipeline proceeds as follows: 1) For each query, sample N diverse candidate reasoning solutions using stochastic decoding with diverse prompting; 2) Elicit pairwise self-preferences from the same model, avoiding the full O(N²) comparison cost by sampling a uniform random spanning tree over N candidates (only N-1 edges, guaranteeing global connectivity while injecting structural randomness) across M independent trials; 3) Aggregate sparse pairwise preferences into a global ranking distribution using Bradley-Terry modeling with PageRank normalization; 4) Decompose structural uncertainty into two complementary components: across-trial ranking instability (fluctuation of rankings across repeated trials, reflecting unstable reasoning processes) and within-trial candidate ambiguity (fuzziness of preferences among candidates within a single trial).1d:T798,The proposed method, Pareto LoRA, treats text loss and image loss as two coupled objectives and seeks a shared descent direction instead of combining them with a fixed scalar weight. Let g_txt and g_img be the gradients of the two modality-specific losses with respect to the trainable LoRA parameters. The method searches for the minimum-norm vector in the convex hull of these gradients. By the MGDA result, this vector either corresponds to a Pareto-stationary point or provides a common descent direction that improves both objectives. Because there are only two objectives, the optimal mixing coefficient has a closed-form solution, which keeps the additional optimization overhead modest.

An important detail is that the method is not applied blindly at every step. The paper first diagnoses the relationship between modality-specific gradients using two signals: cosine similarity for directional conflict and gradient magnitude ratio for dominance. This is a sensible design choice. If the gradients are already aligned and similarly scaled, intervention is unnecessary. The problematic cases are those where one modality overwhelms the update or where the two objectives pull in conflicting directions. This is precisely where fixed loss-level weighting is too crude, because it cannot adapt to layer-wise, stage-wise, or batch-wise changes in gradient geometry.

The LoRA-specific angle also matters. Since LoRA updates only low-rank adapters, adaptation capacity is limited. If the shared update direction is persistently driven by language gradients, the vision side is more likely to be underfit. Pareto LoRA can be interpreted as using that limited adaptation budget more carefully by steering each update toward a more balanced direction. Compared with generic gradient surgery methods, the paper is more targeted: unified multimodal generation, LoRA parameters, and text-image balance rather than arbitrary multi-task settings.1e:T67e,Several key findings are clear from the provided text. First, the paper performs an important control experiment: it fine-tunes unimodal counterparts and compares them against the unified multimodal model on the same data. Vision performance drops much more relative to its unimodal counterpart than text performance does, which makes the imbalance concrete rather than anecdotal. Second, the authors directly inspect modality-specific gradients on shared parameters and find substantial imbalance in three of four evaluated tasks, with gradient magnitudes differing by orders of magnitude. Third, the direction and severity of imbalance vary across tasks, layers, and training stages, which explains why a fixed loss coefficient is often inadequate.

The excerpt does not include the full result tables, so exact model sizes, datasets, image-generation metrics, text metrics, and absolute gains over baselines cannot be stated reliably here. In the full paper, the most important numbers to extract are: (1) the relative performance drop of text and image modalities versus their unimodal counterparts; (2) layer-wise gradient norm ratios; (3) whether Pareto LoRA improves image quality while preserving text performance; and (4) the extra compute cost and any effect on training stability.

For ablations, the most informative comparisons would be: magnitude balancing only, conflict handling only, and the full Pareto integration; as well as performance under different LoRA ranks. If the gains are strongest at low rank, that would support the claim that the method is specifically addressing the capacity bottleneck of parameter-efficient multimodal tuning.1f:T6c8,LiveStarPro is built from three components, with the first two clearly described in the provided text. The first is SVeD, Streaming Verification Decoding. Instead of training the model to emit EOS or silence decisions frame by frame, SVeD uses single-pass perplexity verification to determine whether the current moment warrants a response. The conceptual move is important: silence becomes a derived state rather than a supervised prediction target. This avoids the optimization pathologies of EOS-heavy training while keeping the model focused on meaningful video-language generation.

The second component is a hierarchical memory mechanism for long-horizon streams. The paper criticizes pure sliding-window approaches on three grounds: context saturation is inevitable, FIFO eviction destroys distant but important history, and evicted information becomes unrecoverable. LiveStarPro therefore introduces layered memory with explicit retrieval, separating recent context from longer-term stored information so that the system can recall events far outside the active window. The excerpt does not expose the full memory schema, but the intended design is clear: not all frames should remain in the prompt; some should be compressed, indexed, and retrievable.

The third component appears to be broader training/evaluation support for proactive streaming assistants. The introduction argues that current datasets are too concentrated in egocentric video and that current benchmarks overemphasize synchronous VQA while under-covering live narration, temporal grounding, and multi-turn interaction. The full paper likely includes new data and benchmark assets to support these settings, though the provided text stops before the details.20:T4d0,From the available content, the experimental agenda is clear even though the numbers are missing. The paper is positioned against EOS-based online Video-LLM systems such as VideoLLM-online, VideoLLM-MoD, and LION-FS. The expected comparisons are about response timing quality, long-horizon memory retention, and broader online video understanding tasks. The key claim is that verification-based response triggering is better aligned with the model’s semantics than explicit silence prediction, and that hierarchical memory improves access to distant events.

The excerpt does not contain result tables, metrics, model sizes, or training scales, so no precise figures should be stated. When reading the full paper, four checks matter most. Fourth, what is the latency and compute overhead, since a streaming assistant is only useful if the timing gains do not come with prohibitive runtime cost.21:T5ed,UniAR is a single-tokenizer, autoregressive unified model.
1) Multi-level feature fusion tokenizer: it fuses features from multiple layers of a pretrained vision encoder so shallow layers contribute textures/colors while deeper layers contribute semantics, aiming to serve both discriminative and generative needs.
2) Lookup-free bitwise quantization: continuous features are mapped to binary vectors (e.g., 64-bit) without an explicit codebook. Discrete codes are defined by bit patterns rather than nearest-neighbor lookup in a learned codebook.
3) Parallel bitwise prediction over 2×2 grids: instead of predicting long per-pixel token sequences, it predicts multi-level binary vectors in parallel within each 2×2 spatial block, reducing AR sequence length and claiming ~32× visual compression.
4) DiT-based visual decoder with resolution upsampling: the decoder conditions only on visual tokens (no text prompt) and supports high-resolution reconstruction; the paper claims that 1024×1024 generation requires predicting only 256 visual tokens.
5) Training recipe: three stages (large-scale multimodal pretraining, SFT on curated data, RL for generation). The tokenizer and decoder are frozen throughout; the decoder is only used during RL to decode images for reward computation.
Positioning vs prior work: it relates to Infinity (binary quantization) but argues Infinity is generation/reconstruction-oriented, while UniAR targets unified understanding+generation under standard next-token AR (not next-scale).22:T407,The paper is not merely about faster video generation. It targets a stricter setting: single-GPU, real-time, native streaming, joint audio-video autoregressive generation over long horizons. The motivating domain is social video, where speech rhythm, lip motion, facial expression, ambient sound, and conversational pacing must stay synchronized while the model emits content continuously. Standard bidirectional video diffusion models are a poor fit for this setting for two structural reasons: they are expensive because they denoise large spatiotemporal token grids over many steps, and they are not naturally streamable because an entire clip is generated jointly before frames can be finalized. Prior streaming or autoregressive video generators improve latency, but many are vision-centric, treat audio only as conditioning, drift over long rollouts, or fail to achieve true real-time generation on a single GPU. MaineCoon is positioned as a first prototype for a social world model that addresses these constraints together.23:T5c0,From the visible full text, the central move is to build social video generation as native streaming autoregressive audio-visual generation rather than patching a standard diffusion pipeline. The design appears to have several components. First, generation is causal: the model emits future frames or chunks conditioned on historical context and reuses past states, making it closer to language-model-style rollout than full-clip bidirectional denoising. Second, audio is not just an external condition; it is part of the generated target, so the model must learn a shared temporal representation linking speech content, prosody, lip motion, facial dynamics, and scene evolution. Third, the emphasis on long-horizon continuation and single-GPU real-time inference suggests system-level choices such as caching, chunked context, local attention, or very low-step generation to avoid the scaling bottleneck of full spatiotemporal attention. Fourth, the paper introduces the broader notion of a social world model, where the generator is only the generative core and may later be paired with observation, memory, user-state simulation, and planning modules. Relative to standard video diffusion, the novelty is less about visual quality tuning and more about changing both the training target and inference paradigm toward online interaction. Relative to prior streaming video work, the difference is that audio and social pacing are treated as central rather than auxiliary.24:T42f,The visible text does not include the full experimental tables, so precise model size, data scale, and gains should not be inferred. Based on the introduction, the claimed evaluation targets are: native streaming joint audio-video generation, synchronization between speech and visual motion, long-horizon continuation at minute scale, and real-time generation on a single GPU. These claims need careful verification in the main paper. In particular, the important questions are whether real-time means wall-clock generation faster than playback, whether the single GPU is a datacenter or consumer device, whether long-horizon stability is measured quantitatively rather than shown only in demos, and whether audio-visual synchronization is evaluated with dedicated metrics instead of subjective examples alone. If comparisons are provided against prior streaming video or audio-conditioned video systems, the key point is whether MaineCoon satisfies the full constraint set simultaneously: real-time, single-GPU, joint audio-video generation, and long-horizon stability.25:T5ef,Dynamic Rollout Editing (DRE) has three pillars:
1) Make “answer emergence” observable: for each sampled trajectory, parse intermediate outputs to extract an answer candidate and use an external verifier to find the earliest position where a correct answer can be verified. This splits tokens into a necessary prefix vs. post-answer continuation.
2) Edit successful overthinking rollouts: when a successful rollout contains substantial continuation after emergence, construct an edited counterpart that preserves a conservative verified prefix up to a safe boundary around the emergence point, and replaces/truncates the remaining overthinking segment while keeping the correct answer.
3) Rewire the GRPO preference within the same group: place the original and edited trajectories in the same GRPO group and prefer the shorter edited one, so the positive learning signal is less coupled with post-answer verbosity.
Two training stabilizers are introduced:
- Prefix masking: avoid directly penalizing the shared verified prefix, preventing degradation of the reasoning needed to reach the answer.
- GClip: provide a controlled learning signal around the edit boundary to mitigate instability caused by hard edits near the cut point.
Key difference from alternatives: it is not a global length penalty (which can suppress useful reasoning), and not a post-hoc stopping controller (which does not change what RL reinforces). It explicitly changes the training signal to decouple success from post-answer continuation.26:T5d3,The key construct is Minimal Sufficient CoT (MSC). Given input x, gold answer y*, and a generated CoT trajectory z, the paper defines a sufficiency score Sθ(z|x,y*) based on how well z supports producing y* under the model. Instead of raw joint likelihood (which decays exponentially with answer length), it uses a geometric mean (per-token average) for stability. With a threshold δ, a trajectory prefix is δ-sufficient if Sθ exceeds δ; MSC is the shortest sentence-level prefix that is δ-sufficient, avoiding token-level truncation that can break step structure.

SuCo is a two-stage training recipe:
1) MSC-Aligned Fine-Tuning (MFT): extract MSC prefixes from full-CoT trajectories to build (x, MSC, y*) supervision, then SFT to internalize concise-but-sufficient reasoning patterns.
2) Sufficiency-Aware Policy Optimization (SAPO): RL to learn continuous, difficulty-adaptive reasoning allocation. The paper emphasizes (i) sufficiency-aware rewards that penalize both insufficient reasoning and excessive tokens, and (ii) a dynamic complexity pool that tracks the evolving distribution of reasoning behaviors during training to stabilize sampling and mitigate policy-induced distribution drift.

Compared to prior adaptive reasoning models, SuCo avoids external classifiers/domain labels and avoids discrete budget modes; it operationalizes “when to stop” via sufficiency thresholds and shortest-prefix extraction, then trains a policy to control reasoning length continuously.27:T970,方法核心是ZPPO，建立在GRPO之上，并吸收了DAPO和REINFORCE++里对训练稳定性有帮助的若干recipe。它有三个关键部件。

第一是BCQ，Binary Candidate-included Question。作者先定义hard question：学生在该题上的平均rollout准确率低于0.5。对这类题，构造一个改写后的prompt，在原题后附上两个匿名候选答案，一个来自教师且正确，一个来自学生且错误，顺序打乱，然后要求学生“参考候选但重新作答”。关键点是学生最终输出的答案仍然是自己生成的，不是复制候选，因此policy gradient仍然作用在学生自己的response上，而不是教师response上。和直接把teacher completion当作正样本做RL或SFT不同，这里教师只提供局部支架，不进入梯度路径。

第二是NCQ，Negative Candidate-included Question。作者把同一题上学生此前所有错误回答显式列出来，并告诉模型这些都错了，再要求重新作答。它利用了一个很有意思的训练信号：在普通group rollout里，不同采样彼此不可见，错误只是独立失败；而NCQ把这些失败聚合成共享负信号，让模型第一次“看到自己都错在了哪里”。这不是教师监督，而是把学生自己的失败变成可利用上下文。

第三是prompt replay buffer。作者只缓存难题prompt，不缓存response。难题会被反复采样训练，直到该题rollout准确率升到0.5以上才“毕业”，否则在buffer满时按FIFO淘汰。这个设计对应文中的zone of proximal development：只反复练那些学生暂时不会、但在少量帮助下有机会学会的题。只存prompt而不存答案，也是在避免把训练退化成离线模仿。

训练底座方面，作者使用GRPO，并加入DAPO的clip-higher和token-level policy gradient loss。同时采用REINFORCE++里两个看似简单但对小模型很敏感的设置：每步更多iterations，以及batch-level advantage normalization，并且把zero-advantage group排除在归一化统计之外。文中强调，这些recipe如果处理不好，会带来相当明显的性能回落。方法上的区别也很清楚：它不同于KD，因为不拟合teacher distribution；不同于on-policy distillation，因为teacher不直接参与student action的梯度；也不同于纯self-improvement，因为hard case上额外引入了教师支架。28:T979,ZPPO is built on top of GRPO, with additional training choices borrowed from DAPO and REINFORCE++. The method has three main components.

First, BCQ, or Binary Candidate-included Question. A question is marked hard when the student’s mean rollout accuracy is below 0.5. For such questions, the prompt is reformulated by appending two anonymized candidate answers: one correct teacher answer and one incorrect student answer, shuffled in order. The student is then asked to solve the problem again while consulting the candidates. The key point is that the student still generates its own final response. The policy gradient is applied only to the student’s generated answer, not to the teacher answer. This is the central mechanism behind “teacher in prompts, not gradients.”

Second, NCQ, or Negative Candidate-included Question. The prompt includes all wrong student responses collected for the same question, explicitly marks them as incorrect, and asks the student to answer again. This turns independent failures into a shared negative signal. In ordinary grouped rollouts, failed attempts do not see one another. NCQ is the first place where the model can condition on its own collective mistakes.

Third, a prompt replay buffer. Only hard prompts are stored, not responses. These prompts are repeatedly sampled until they “graduate,” meaning rollout accuracy reaches at least 0.5, or until they are evicted by FIFO when the buffer is full. This operationalizes the “zone of proximal development” idea: repeatedly practice questions that are currently unsolved but potentially learnable with limited guidance. Storing prompts without responses is also a deliberate choice to avoid drifting into offline imitation.

On the optimization side, the method uses GRPO with DAPO’s clip-higher and token-level policy gradient loss, plus two recipe choices highlighted from REINFORCE++: more iterations per step and batch-level advantage normalization, with zero-advantage groups excluded from the normalization statistics. The paper argues that these details matter materially in the small-model regime. Relative to prior work, ZPPO differs from distillation because it does not fit teacher distributions; from on-policy distillation because the teacher response is not part of the student gradient target; and from pure self-improvement because hard cases still receive external scaffolding from the teacher.29:T85f,The experimental focus is not frontier-scale performance but knowledge transfer and generalization for small students. The method is instantiated on small Qwen3.5-family models, with particular emphasis on capacity-limited students such as 0.8B and 2B. The tasks are reasoning-heavy, especially math-style settings, and the evaluation separates in-distribution gains from generalization to benchmark families beyond the training corpus.

Three result patterns appear central. First, ZPPO improves out-of-distribution generalization more reliably than distillation-style baselines and standard RL. The paper’s argument is that distillation can fit teacher behavior near the training distribution but becomes brittle beyond it, whereas ZPPO lets the student solve with prompt-level support rather than distribution-level imitation.

Second, the hard-question machinery is doing real work. Ablations removing BCQ, NCQ, or the prompt replay buffer all hurt performance, with BCQ and replay typically carrying most of the gain and NCQ adding complementary signal. The paper also analyzes zero-advantage groups directly: under standard RL they contribute almost nothing, while ZPPO converts many of them into learnable training cases through prompt reformulation.

Third, training recipe details matter a lot for small models. Iterations per step, batch-level advantage normalization, and excluding zero-advantage groups from normalization statistics account for a meaningful fraction of the total gain. That is an important practical point: the improvement is not only from adding teacher hints, but also from stabilizing the RL dynamics of small students.

The provided full-text excerpt does not include the complete result tables, so it would be unsafe to invent exact numbers. What is clear is the comparison set: distillation, on-policy or self-distillation style baselines, and pure RL baselines; and the evaluation axes: generalization, hard-question learning, and component ablations. When reading the paper, the main tables and the 0.8B-student results deserve the closest attention, since that is where the paper’s claim is strongest.2a:T68d,Read it around the failure modes, not around the method name. Start with the introduction and the overview figures. The paper is easiest to understand once two rejected options are clear: logit imitation is problematic for small students, and directly using teacher responses inside the policy gradient creates off-policy drift.

Then go to the formal definitions of BCQ, NCQ, and the prompt replay buffer. The most important implementation question is what exactly receives gradient and what remains context only. That is where the paper’s main claim lives. Many methods in this area sound like RL but quietly rely on offline supervision; this paper is trying hard not to do that.

Next, read the ablations carefully. Prioritize the effect of removing BCQ, NCQ, and replay; the sensitivity to the hard-question threshold at 0.5; the role of replay capacity and eviction; and whether zero-advantage groups are included in normalization statistics. In small-model training, these details often matter more than the algorithm label.

Finally, focus on the generalization results rather than only in-distribution metrics. The paper’s main argument is not just higher reward, but less brittleness than distillation beyond the training corpus. If there is an error analysis section, it is worth checking whether the student is truly reasoning better or merely learning to choose among candidates.

If time is limited, a good path is: introduction, method figure, main result table, and key ablation table. That should take about 30 to 45 minutes. If you want to assess transferability to your own training stack, then read the implementation appendix and hyperparameter details.2b:T842,The method, VEPA (Visual Evidence Pre-Alignment), factorizes answering into two steps. First, the MLLM generates textual visual evidence conditioned on the image and the question. Second, a frozen blind reader—an LLM that does not see the image—receives only the question and the generated evidence, and tries to recover the ground-truth answer. In probabilistic terms, the paper rewrites direct answering as a decomposition through a latent bottleneck e: an evidence policy P(e|v,q) followed by a reasoning policy P(a|e,q). The bottleneck is intentional. It forces the model to externalize the visual content that is actually needed for answering.

The optimization target is built around two properties. Sufficiency means the evidence should contain enough information for the blind reader to infer the answer. Visual dependence means the evidence must genuinely come from the image rather than from question priors or direct answer leakage. To train the evidence policy, the paper uses GRPO. For each sample, the model generates a group of candidate evidence sequences, and learning is driven by relative rewards within the group.

The reward design is the critical part. The paper does not simply reward the reader for getting the answer right. It adds constraints to prevent degenerate solutions: penalties against answer leakage, so the evidence cannot collapse into a disguised answer channel; penalties against repetitive or low-information evidence, so RL does not reward verbosity or templated outputs; and a decoupling between grounding and final answer generation, so the optimization pressure stays on evidence quality rather than downstream response style. This is the main difference from direct answer-level RL, where the model can still exploit shortcuts from language priors. Compared with replacing captions by dense descriptions, VEPA is also different in supervision structure: the evidence is question-conditioned, not a static attempt to describe the whole image. That makes it closer to actual inference-time information needs and more bandwidth-efficient than generic dense captioning.2c:T55f,From the available full-text content, the paper reports consistent gains over the standard training recipe across several visually demanding settings, including knowledge-intensive VQA, compositional VQA, fine-grained perception, and broader multimodal evaluation. The improvements are said to hold across different backbones, which matters because the claim is about training strategy rather than a backbone-specific trick.

The excerpt does not include the full result tables, model sizes, or dataset scales, so precise numbers should not be invented. Still, the experimental story is clear. First, the frozen blind reader is important because it turns answer supervision into a sufficiency test for evidence, instead of letting the same model self-validate. Second, anti-leakage reward terms are necessary; otherwise the evidence channel can degenerate into direct answer transmission. Third, the gains transfer to out-of-distribution settings, which supports the claim that VEPA improves grounding rather than merely fitting benchmark-specific answer patterns. When reading the full paper, the most important checks are: how gains distribute across perception-heavy versus reasoning-heavy benchmarks, how strong the ablations are for each reward component, and whether the qualitative evidence examples truly show better visual extraction rather than just longer outputs.2d:T469,The proposed Experiential Latent Memory (ELM) pipeline creates a small latent memory after each solved problem. Instead of updating the backbone, it trains a tiny soft-prompt-like module (claimed ~0.001% of parameters) with a few gradient steps, using self-generated signals from the model’s own reasoning trajectories (success/failure exploration under test-time compute).
The paper’s design logic is:
1) Textual trace replay is bottlenecked: raw CoT traces (even with reflection-style refinement) are surface artifacts and do not reliably transfer; hidden states may contain more abstract structure than what is verbalized.
2) Internal-reward learning can provide a learning signal without labels, but prior approaches are mostly offline and can degrade as more test data accumulates.
3) Modularity isolates updates: per-instance memories are stored separately and composed at retrieval time, aiming to mitigate interference and forgetting compared to full-parameter RL/finetuning.
Operationally it looks like retrieval (select relevant memories) + injection (compose soft prompts) + tiny online optimization (memory-only).2e:T43e,The evaluation uses two scenarios. For biohazardous knowledge, the authors filter WMDP biology to 189 targeted multiple-choice questions, generate three paraphrases per question, use all paraphrases for unlearning, split questions for relearning versus held-out MCQ evaluation, and use domain-matched biology text from FineFineWeb as retain data. For harmful behavioral tendency removal, they use the animal_abuse category from BeaverTails and construct a synthetic retain set by replacing harmful concepts with benign counterparts while keeping surrounding context. The models include Llama 3.1 8B, Qwen 3.5 9B, Gemma 4 E4B, and DeepSeek-V2-Lite, covering dense and MoE families. Methods are compared under a fixed disruption budget: training stops once KL(pθ0∥pθ) on WikiText exceeds 0.01, roughly a 0.4% NLL increase. RepSelect reports an 8–50× larger post-relearning answer-probability reduction than the best baseline, NPO, on biohazardous knowledge, and a 4–15× larger reduction than NPO or RMU on animal-abuse tendencies, while matching general capability preservation.2f:T6b8,The method has two parts: diagnosis and correction. For diagnosis, the authors project hidden states from each layer into output probabilities and track how answer preference evolves with depth. They define Modal Dominance Ratio (MDR) to quantify whether a layer prefers the image-supported answer w_vis or the text-supported answer w_text. This yields a layerwise trajectory of modal preference. In many conflict-incorrect examples, MDR is positive in intermediate layers, indicating visual preference, and turns negative only in later layers, indicating a shift toward text. The authors further show that the mere existence of a late shift is not enough to predict failure, because successful examples also shift. What matters is the direction of the shift: failures usually move toward text, while successes usually move toward vision. For correction, they propose Conflict-Aware Layer Reference Decoding (CALRD). The method identifies the transition layer where the output distribution changes most sharply and treats it as an anchor layer that still contains the pre-override prediction. It then computes two signals: anchor confidence, which measures how certain the anchor-layer prediction is, and prediction retention, which measures whether that prediction survives to the final layer. When the anchor is confident but poorly retained, CALRD interprets this as harmful override and blends anchor-layer logits back into the final distribution with adaptive strength. When late-layer processing appears beneficial, it leaves the output mostly unchanged. This is more selective than naive multi-layer ensembling because it aims to correct only harmful overwriting rather than weakening deep computation in general.30:T4e2,The paper introduces Conflict-VQA, a diagnostic benchmark with 5,969 samples and explicit annotations for both the image-supported answer and the text-supported answer. Each image-question pair is paired with both consistent and conflicting contexts. Images come from VrR-VG and questions from TDIUC, covering six categories: object presence, color, attribute, counting, spatial reasoning, and activity recognition. Two headline mechanistic numbers are central: among failures, 85% of prediction shifts move toward text, while among successes, 89% move toward vision. This shows that shift direction is more informative than shift occurrence. The evaluation spans five MLLMs, from InstructBLIP to Qwen3-VL, suggesting that the phenomenon is not architecture-specific. CALRD yields up to 9.4% improvement on conflict tasks while largely preserving standard performance on non-conflict settings. When reading the full paper, the most important experimental checks are: whether MDR trajectories are consistent across model families, whether anchor confidence and prediction retention each contribute in ablations, whether non-adaptive blending hurts clean examples, and whether the same pattern holds beyond Conflict-VQA on existing conflict benchmarks.31:T432,This is worth a careful skim, close to a light deep read. Start with the analysis section and make sure the definition of MDR is clear, because it is the backbone of the paper. Then inspect the layerwise trajectory figures that show the model initially favoring the visual answer before drifting toward text. After that, read the CALRD equations and pseudocode. The important point is not the blending operation itself, but how the method distinguishes harmful override from beneficial late-layer refinement. In the experiments, prioritize the Conflict-VQA construction, the main results table, and the ablations on the two diagnostic signals. Around 30-45 minutes should be enough to capture the main contribution. If your interest is in multimodal pretraining or post-training, two follow-up questions are worth thinking about: whether the bias is rooted mainly in language-heavy pretraining priors or in instruction tuning that over-rewards textual compliance, and whether this late-layer override can be reduced during training rather than only patched at decoding time.32:T471,The paper introduces a dual diagnostic framework: layer-wise linear probes measure when the answer is externally recoverable (availability), while Context-Stripped Decoding (CSD) tests whether the representation at a given layer is already sufficient for the model itself to decode the answer after stripping downstream contextual leakage (readiness). Using this lens, the authors describe a brewing-to-resolution lifecycle and partition samples into four outcomes: Resolved (answer is brewed and then correctly read out), Overprocessed (a usable answer forms early but is degraded in later layers), Misresolved (the model brews into a wrong answer and stabilizes it), and Unresolved (no usable answer forms by the final layer). They design six synthetic code task families—Value Tracking, Computing, Conditional, Function Call, Loop, Loop-unrolled—with single-digit outputs, ensuring a one-token target across tokenizers and a unified 11-way diagnostic space. Causal interventions include activation patching at the jointly correct layer, layer skipping for Overprocessed cases, and representation re-injection for Unresolved cases.33:T43d,The study covers 16 decoder-only Transformers from 0.5B to 14B parameters across Qwen2.5-Coder, Qwen2.5, Qwen3, DeepSeek-Coder, CodeLlama, and Llama-2, with 24,300 samples per model in the main setting (4,050 per task family, 150 per configuration). The main text focuses on Qwen2.5-Coder-7B. Only 41.5% of samples are Resolved overall; three of six task families fall below 30% Resolved: Computing, Function Call, and Loop-unrolled. Value Tracking reaches 70.8% Resolved while semantically related Loop-unrolled reaches only 28.0%. For Function Call, Resolved rate drops from 61.1% at depth 1 to 2.5% at depth 3. Across models, the brewing scaffold is relatively stable in normalized duration (about 24–42% of total layers), whereas resolution success varies substantially with capability, scale, and training. Causal interventions validate the taxonomy: Overprocessed samples benefit from layer skipping, while Unresolved samples can be improved by re-injecting activations from the correct layer, indicating intervention-sensitive computational states rather than post hoc labels.34:T405,The model is a 1-layer decoder-only Transformer with d_model=128, 4 attention heads, head dimension 32, MLP width 512, ReLU activations, and no LayerNorm. Inputs are formatted as [a, b, =], and the output is read at the equals position. The training set contains 30% of the 12,544 nonzero input pairs, or 3,763 examples, with the remaining 70% used for testing. Training uses AdamW with learning rate 1e-3, weight decay 1.0, full-batch updates, and 40,000 epochs. The model memorizes the training set around epoch 500, while test loss stays high; between roughly epochs 9,000 and 14,000, test loss drops to near zero. In the raw additive Fourier basis, the embedding spectrum is almost uniform, with Gini coefficient 0.071. In the multiplicative character basis, energy concentrates on four key frequencies, {2, 8, 47, 56}, with Gini coefficient 0.579, about 8.1× higher. Neuron activation patterns cluster more cleanly by frequency in the multiplicative basis, and SVD components become sinusoidal when ordered by discrete log.35:T453,The analysis considers two-task continual regression. After training on Task A, the model is at θ_A; Task B training starts from this point. The model is linearized around θ_A, and the Task-A prediction shift induced by Task-B training is derived in closed form: Δf_A ≈ -K_AB (K_BB + λI)^-1 r_B. K_AB is the cross-task NTK between Task-A probe inputs and Task-B training inputs, K_BB is the Task-B NTK, and r_B is the Task-B residual evaluated at θ_A. The important operational detail is that all terms are computed before Task-B optimization. In frozen-backbone linear-head PEFT, the model is exactly linear in the trainable parameters, so the predictor is exact up to numerical precision. For LoRA, adapters, and full fine-tuning, it becomes a local NTK approximation. The paper then decomposes the shift in the eigenbasis of K_AA and shows that the forgetting vector lies in the image of the Task-A Jacobian. For a frozen backbone with a trainable shared linear head, K_AA admits a Kronecker structure, yielding an effective vulnerable rank on the order of C times the feature-Gram effective rank.36:T426,The paper restricts outputs to a discrete answer set A (multiple-choice), enabling exact computation of predictive probabilities from logits rather than sampling-based density estimates.
It introduces Prompted Predictive Resampling (PPR): the model generates a sequence of answers to the same question within a single prompted interaction; at each step, the predictive distribution over A is recomputed, yielding a belief trajectory. Martingale/c.i.d. coherence is then tested on this trajectory.
Empirical pattern: early steps show belief drift (martingale violations), but after enough resampling steps the belief process self-stabilizes and converges to a coherent regime.
Two practical add-ons are proposed: (i) a prompting trick to accelerate stabilization; (ii) an amortized finetuning strategy with a loss derived from enforcing c.i.d., aiming to get the stabilized distribution in fewer (ideally one) steps, improving calibration without hurting accuracy.
Overall: a diagnostic protocol + an inference-time procedure + an optional finetuning regularizer.37:T5b3,Built on the Qwen3-VL backbone, Qwen-RobotNav unifies all navigation tasks as waypoint trajectory prediction and exposes a two-dimensional parameterized interface. First, task modes (VLN, PointNav, ObjNav, Tracking) allow the upper planner to select appropriate navigation behavior. Second, controllable observation parameters (visual token budget, temporal decay, per-camera importance weights) govern how the visual stream is encoded. All interface parameters are randomized during training to prevent overfitting to fixed configurations, enabling generalization to any inference-time setup without task-specific fine-tuning. Camera identity, temporal order, and embodiment type are passed entirely via natural language tags and prompt preambles, with no modifications to the base backbone architecture. A two-tier hierarchical agent system is also designed: the upper-tier Qwen3.6-Plus planner decomposes complex goals into sub-goals and dispatches configurable navigation calls, while Qwen-RobotNav acts as a reactive executor for high-frequency waypoint prediction. A two-level memory system (compact single-episode rollout summary + persistent cross-episode memory storing searched regions, candidate object locations, etc.) supports long-horizon reasoning. The training dataset totals 15.6M samples: 85% is navigation trajectory planning data across 5 task families and diverse embodiments, and 15% is navigation-related vision-language reasoning data.3:["$","main",null,{"className":"flex-1 overflow-y-auto","children":["$","div",null,{"className":"mx-auto max-w-4xl px-4 py-8 sm:px-6 xl:max-w-6xl","children":["$","$L9",null,{"data":{"date":"2026-06-18","generated_at":"2026-06-18T01:52:06.556104+00:00","stats":{"total":114,"starred":2,"must_read":9,"skim":49,"glance":56,"by_category":{"pretraining_data":3,"industry":1,"llm_pretrain":2,"scaling":2,"architecture":11,"efficient":12,"eval":16,"multimodal":10,"multimodal_gen":9,"post_train":17,"mechanism":16,"coding_agent":2,"agent":13},"fetch_status":{"arxiv":"ok","huggingface":"ok","blog":"ok","twitter":"empty","github_trending":"ok","hf_trending":"ok"}},"categories":[{"id":"pretraining_data","emoji":"📚","name":"预训练数据","description":"以预训练数据为核心贡献的工作：数据采集/清洗/去重、数据配比(data mixture)、数据筛选/质量评估、语料构建、数据课程(data curriculum)、数据重复(repetition)、合成数据用于预训练、Tokenizer/词表设计、成员推断/数据检测。判据是『论文核心是数据本身』，而非训练 recipe/优化器(→ llm_pretrain)或评测资产(→ eval)","papers":[{"id":"2606.18192","title":"The Stanford EDGAR Filings Dataset: Reconstructing U.S. Corporate and Financial Disclosures into Layout-Faithful and Token-Efficient Pretraining Data","abstract":"As high-quality public web corpora become increasingly exhausted, clean long-context documents have become a scarce and expensive source of training data for large language models (LLMs). Ensuring the privacy of such data against extraction attacks has become a central concern. In this paper, we ask whether an attacker who can poison a portion of the training data can facilitate the leakage of a separate target record they have no access to. We answer in the affirmative and show that such leakage can be induced by","starred":false,"priority":"p2","reading_tier":"skim","source":"arxiv","sources":["arxiv"],"published_at":"2026-06-15T07:04:01Z","category_id":"pretraining_data","cover_url":"/covers/2606_17110.png","hf_upvotes":0,"arxiv_categories":["cs.CR","cs.LG"],"summary":{"core_problem":"攻击者能否通过在训练集中注入少量投毒数据，迫使模型在训练过程中强行记忆（memorize）其原本无法访问的、其他用户的隐私数据（如 PII），从而在推理阶段实现定向提取？","importance":"揭示了一种全新的、针对预训练/微调阶段的数据投毒攻击范式（Loss Landscape Poisoning, LLP）。该研究证明了即使在标准的 DP-SGD（差分隐私）保护下，虽然直接生成隐私数据被阻断，但攻击者仍能通过黑盒 Loss 探测（DLRP）提取出隐私数据。这挑战了当前 LLM 隐私保护的基石，表明仅靠梯度裁剪和加噪无法防御基于损失地形（Loss Landscape）的攻击。","technical_approach":"核心机制是重塑目标隐私数据周围的局部损失地形（Loss Landscape）。攻击者构造与目标隐私数据结构相似（如相同的表单模板）但关键字段（如社保号）为随机值的“诱饵”数据。在训练时，通过模型投毒（白盒，修改诱饵数据的 Loss 为梯度上升）或数据投毒（黑盒，仅注入诱饵数据），人为抬高目标隐私数据邻域的 Loss。当模型正常训练到真实的隐私数据时，由于周围邻域 Loss 被人为垫高，模型无法通过平滑泛化来拟合，被迫在真实隐私数据处形成一个极其陡峭的局部极小值（sharp local minimum），从而导致强记忆。针对 DP-SGD 的防御，作者提出了 Direct Loss Region Probing (DLRP) 攻击：通过黑盒查询候选隐私数据的 Loss 及其微小扰动后的 Loss 变化，利用真实隐私数据处于“陡峭极小值”的特性（即扰动后 Loss 必然显著上升）来锁定真实数据。","experiments":"在白盒模型投毒下，目标数据被直接生成的概率从基线（0.01-0.04）飙升至 0.7-0.92，在 LLM 上甚至达到 100%。在更现实的黑盒数据投毒（LLP-Data）下，LLM 上的泄露率也达到了 86%。在 DP-SGD 防御下，虽然直接生成攻击失效，但 DLRP 攻击仍能在保持模型可用性的隐私预算下成功提取目标数据。","reading_guide":"重点阅读 Introduction 中对 Loss Landscape 重塑机制的直观解释（Figure 1），以及 DLRP 攻击如何绕过 DP-SGD 的分析。这对于理解模型记忆机制和隐私保护的局限性非常有启发。","authors_note":""},"summary_en":{"core_problem":"Can an attacker inject a small amount of poisoned data into the training set to force the model to memorize and subsequently leak targeted private data (e.g., PII) belonging to other users, which the attacker has no direct access to?","importance":"This paper introduces a novel data poisoning paradigm, Loss Landscape Poisoning (LLP), targeting the pre-training/fine-tuning phase. It demonstrates that even under standard Differential Privacy (DP-SGD) protection, which blocks direct generation, attackers can still extract private data via black-box loss probing (DLRP). This challenges the foundation of current LLM privacy defenses, showing that gradient clipping and noise addition are insufficient against landscape-level attacks.","technical_approach":"The core mechanism involves reshaping the local loss landscape around the targeted private data. The attacker crafts 'decoy' data sharing the same structure (e.g., a form template) as the target but with random values in the sensitive field. During training, either through model poisoning (white-box, applying gradient ascent to decoys) or data poisoning (black-box, injecting decoys), the loss in the neighborhood of the target data is artificially elevated. When the model encounters the actual private data, the elevated surrounding loss prevents smooth generalization, forcing the model to create a sharp local minimum and strongly memorize the target. To bypass DP-SGD, the authors propose Direct Loss Region Probing (DLRP): by querying the black-box loss of candidate secrets and their perturbed versions, the attacker identifies the true secret by its characteristic sharp minimum (where any perturbation causes a significant loss increase).","experiments":"Under white-box model poisoning, the probability of directly generating the target data jumps from a baseline of 0.01-0.04 to 0.7-0.92, reaching up to 100% on LLMs. In the more realistic black-box data poisoning scenario (LLP-Data), the leakage rate remains high at 86% on LLMs. Crucially, under DP-SGD, while direct generation fails, the DLRP attack successfully extracts the target data at privacy budgets that maintain model utility.","reading_guide":"Focus on the intuitive explanation of the loss landscape reshaping mechanism in the Introduction (Figure 1) and the analysis of how DLRP bypasses DP-SGD. Although progress is usually attributed to scale, data and architecture, we show that parameter initialization is a gene-like determinant of training and, in particular, of model capacity. Reducing the initialization scale consistently improves pretraining, with the largest gains on reasoning-demanding tasks. We identify two widely used empirical settings that","starred":false,"priority":"p2","reading_tier":"must_read","source":"arxiv","sources":["arxiv"],"published_at":"2026-06-16T13:53:48Z","category_id":"llm_pretrain","cover_url":"/covers/2606_17945.png","hf_upvotes":0,"arxiv_categories":["cs.AI"],"summary":{"core_problem":"参数初始化长期被深度学习领域视为已有成熟启发式方案（Xavier/Kaiming）的固定环节，此前关于小初始化有助于推理与泛化的结论多来自小模型或合成任务，尚未在真实LLM预训练场景下完成系统验证：小初始化的收益是否随模型规模成立、为什么之前观察到收益随模型增大而衰减、初始化与现有通用架构组件存在何种交互、最优初始化尺度是多少，这些问题均缺少明确的实证答案。","importance":"该工作将初始化尺度从默认的工程启发式选项，明确为可定量调控的预训练设计轴。研究指出两个被广泛使用的架构配置（RMSNorm的稳定性常数、原生注意力的sink效应）会系统性掩盖小初始化的收益，修正这两个配置可以几乎零额外成本获得稳定的预训练损失下降，且增益集中在推理类任务上。研究提出的γ=1初始化临界点提供了可直接落地的训练配置参考，同时将此前仅在小模型上观察到的权重凝聚训练动态推广到了真实LLM预训练场景，为理解大模型的特征学习过程提供了新的观测维度。","technical_approach":"研究将每层权重矩阵初始化为零均值高斯分布，标准差设为d_in^{-γ}：其中γ=0.5对应主流使用的Xavier初始化尺度，γ越大初始权重的绝对值越小。首先通过控制变量实验，扫描0.1B到1.5B规模LLM在不同γ下的预训练表现，确认小初始化可降低验证损失但收益随模型增大快速衰减的现象。随后逐一定位压制小初始化收益的架构因素：第一，RMSNorm中的稳定性常数ε会在隐状态方差随小初始化降低到与ε同量级时进入饱和区间，此时归一化操作不再对权重尺度变化敏感，完全掩盖初始化带来的差异；第二，小初始化会加剧注意力sink现象（注意力权重过度集中在序列首位token），引入门控注意力结构可缓解这一问题。同时解除两个限制（将ε降低到合适量级、使用门控注意力）后，小初始化在大模型上的收益得以恢复，呈现更优的规模scaling趋势。研究进一步通过权重动态测量和token级损失分解，确认γ=1为最优初始化点——此时残差通路与恒等通路的贡献达到平衡；小初始化模型的训练轨迹呈现“先凝聚后展开”的特征：训练前期权重首先收敛到低维子空间，后期再逐步扩展到高维结构，损失增益集中在需要推理的高难度token上。","experiments":"标准架构配置（ε=1e-5，原生注意力）下，γ=1（小初始化）相比γ=0.5（标准Xavier），0.1B模型验证损失降低约0.05，但1.5B模型上损失差仅为0.003，收益随模型增大显著衰减。针对RMSNorm ε的消融实验显示：1.5B模型、γ=1配置下，将ε从常用的1e-5降到1e-12，验证损失降低约0.038；同样的ε调整在γ=0.5配置下仅带来约0.001的损失变化，几乎无影响。定量计算验证了饱和效应的存在：隐层维度d=2048时，γ=0.5对应的隐状态方差为4.8e-4，远大于1e-5；γ=1对应的隐状态方差为2.4e-7，远小于1e-5，此时归一化完全由ε主导。两个修复措施（低ε、门控注意力）结合后，小初始化的收益随模型增大不再衰减，scaling曲线斜率优于标准配置，下游测试中推理类任务的增益最为显著。γ值扫描实验显示γ=1为性能最优点，平衡了训练稳定性和推理能力，进一步增大γ（初始化尺度过小）会引发训练不稳定。权重动态测量显示，γ=1初始化的模型训练中前期权重有效秩显著低于标准初始化模型，呈现明显的凝聚特征；token级损失分解显示，简单高频token上不同初始化的损失差异极小，主要收益来自高损失、语义复杂的推理类token。","reading_guide":"重点关注Result章节中RMSNorm ε饱和效应的定量推导与对应实验（Figure 1c/d/e），这部分是最易落地的结论——调整ε几乎无实现成本，却能解锁小初始化的潜在收益。其次关注初始化的γ参数化方案与γ=1平衡点的分析，这是对现有默认初始化配置的直接修正。目前公开版本内容在第二个障碍（注意力sink）的分析处截断，关于门控注意力的完整消融可跟进后续正式版本。值得深挖的方向包括：γ=1的最优设置在7B及更大规模模型、MoE/SSM等非Transformer架构上的迁移性，ε最优取值与隐层维度、初始化γ的定量关系，训练前期权重凝聚过程和下游推理能力的因果关联。","authors_note":""},"summary_en":{"core_problem":"Parameter initialization has long been treated as a settled engineering heuristic in deep learning, with Xavier/Kaiming scaling as the de facto standard for LLM training. Prior work demonstrating benefits of small initialization for reasoning and generalization was largely limited to small models and synthetic tasks. It remains untested at production LLM scale whether these benefits hold, why observed gains diminish as model size grows, how initialization interacts with common architectural components, and what initialization scale is optimal for practical pretraining.","importance":"This work establishes initialization scale as an explicitly tunable design axis for LLM pretraining, rather than a fixed default. It shows that two ubiquitous architectural choices — the epsilon constant in RMSNorm, and vanilla attention prone to sink effects — systematically mask the benefits of small initialization. Adjusting these components delivers consistent pretraining loss improvements with near-zero implementation cost, with disproportionate gains on reasoning-intensive tasks. The identified γ=1 initialization point provides a concrete, actionable default for future training, and connects large-model training dynamics to the condensed feature learning regime previously documented only in small networks.","technical_approach":"$c","experiments":"$d","reading_guide":"Prioritize the analysis of RMSNorm epsilon saturation, including the quantitative derivation and Figure 1 panels c/d/e — this is the most immediately actionable finding, as epsilon adjustment requires no code complexity or overhead. The discussion of the attention sink barrier is truncated in the available preprint version; readers may refer to the final publication for complete ablations on gated attention. VoidPad","starred":false,"priority":"p2","reading_tier":"must_read","source":"arxiv","sources":["arxiv"],"published_at":"2026-06-16T14:46:53Z","category_id":"architecture","cover_url":"/covers/2606_17999.png","hf_upvotes":0,"arxiv_categories":["cs.CL"],"summary":{"core_problem":"Masked Diffusion Language Model（MDLM）在 instruction tuning 时常沿用 AR 的做法：用重复的 [EOS] 做 padding。由于 MDLM 是双向条件建模，padding token 会进入训练目标并在推理时参与上下文，导致 [EOS] 同时承担“语义终止符”和“长度/填充符”两种角色。论文指出这会在大 block decoding 下触发 [EOS] overflow：尾部高置信 [EOS] 被写入上下文后进一步抬高邻近 [EOS] 概率，形成自强化，最终输出过短且充满 [EOS]。要解决的是：把终止语义与 padding 长度信号解耦，让 [EOS] 只表达语义终止。","importance":"这是一个典型的“tokenizer/特殊符号语义设计 + 训练-推理耦合”问题，对 diffusion/any-order LM 的指令微调与解码稳定性很关键。贡献不在于提出新的 MDLM 目标，而是指出一个工程上很容易忽略、但会系统性破坏大 block 解码的根因，并给出几乎零成本的修复：新增 [VOID] 作为 padding。对做 MDLM 指令化、追求大 block 并行解码吞吐的人，属于直接可用的 recipe。","technical_approach":"VoidPadding 是训练-推理协同设计：\n1) 训练数据格式改造：把每个样本的 response 组织为 [response] [EOS] [VOID]*，保证每条样本只有一个 [EOS]（语义终止），其后全部用 [VOID] 补齐长度。这样 [EOS] 不再承担 padding 角色，[VOID] 承担长度/尾部信号。\n2) 推理时禁止生成 [VOID]：避免模型把 padding token 当作可输出内容。\n3) [EOS]Termination 早停规则：当“左侧最早可见的 [EOS] 之前已经没有 mask”时停止解码。直觉是：一旦模型在某个位置明确给出终止符，并且终止符之前的内容都已确定，就不需要继续在终止符之后填充，从机制上切断 overflow 的自强化链。\n4) 可选的 VoidExpansion：利用尾部 [VOID] 的长度信号判断 canvas 是否不足；若不足，在去噪前扩展 response canvas，再继续 denoise。它把“长度不足”与“应该终止”分成两种不同 token 的统计信号。\n机制分析部分的关键点是对比 AR vs MDLM：AR 的因果 mask 让前文看不到 padding [EOS]，所以 [EOS] 主要学终止；MDLM 双向注意力让 padding [EOS] 既是条件又是预测目标，训练中会把“尾部大量 [EOS]”学成强先验，推理时在大 block 并行提交下更容易被放大。","experiments":"在 Dream-7B-Instruct 上，论文报告 VoidPadding 能：\n- 显著缓解/消除大 block decoding 下的 [EOS] overflow（定性例子是短回答+一串 [EOS] 的失败模式消失）。\n- 提升 block-size-averaged 的多任务均值表现，并提升解码效率（通过 [EOS]Termination 早停减少无效去噪步）。\n- 对不同 block size、不同 canvas length 更稳健。\n（注：你提供的全文片段只到方法与分析开头，未包含具体任务列表、指标与数值表；精确提升幅度需要对照实验章节的表格与消融。）","reading_guide":"精读建议：\n- 先看“[EOS] overflow 分钟。","authors_note":""},"summary_en":{"core_problem":"Instruction-tuned masked diffusion LMs often inherit the autoregressive convention of padding with repeated [EOS]. In MDLMs, bidirectional conditioning makes padding tokens part of both the context and the prediction targets, so [EOS] ends up playing two roles: semantic termination and padding/length signaling. Under large-block decoding, this dual role can trigger an [EOS] overflow feedback loop: high-confidence tail [EOS] tokens become context, which further increases nearby [EOS] probabilities, yielding overly short answers followed by repeated [EOS]. The paper aims to decouple termination semantics from padding.","importance":"This is a high-leverage training/inference interface fix for diffusion/any-order LMs: a special-token semantics issue that becomes a decoding stability failure at scale (large blocks, parallel commitment). The contribution is not a new diffusion objective, but a minimal recipe—introducing a dedicated padding token—that improves robustness and efficiency for instruction-tuned MDLM deployments. If you are pushing MDLM throughput via large-block decoding, this addresses a concrete, reproducible failure mode.","technical_approach":"VoidPadding is a lightweight co-design:\n1) Training formatting: represent each response as [response] [EOS] [VOID]*, ensuring exactly one [EOS] per example (semantic terminator) and using [VOID] exclusively for padding.\n2) Inference constraint: ban generating [VOID] so padding tokens never appear in outputs.\n3) [EOS]Termination early stopping: stop decoding once there are no remaining masks before the leftmost visible [EOS]. This prevents the model from spending denoising steps on positions that are semantically beyond termination and breaks the self-reinforcing [EOS] overflow loop.\n4) Optional VoidExpansion: use tail [VOID] signals as a length indicator to expand an insufficient response canvas before denoising.\nThe analysis hinges on AR vs MDLM: causal masking in AR prevents earlier tokens from conditioning on padded [EOS], while MDLM’s bidirectional attention makes padded [EOS] a strong learned prior and an active context feature, which becomes brittle under large-block parallel decoding.","experiments":"On Dream-7B-Instruct, the paper reports that VoidPadding prevents or substantially reduces [EOS] overflow, improves block-size-averaged multi-task performance, and increases decoding efficiency via early stopping.
